 PDS, India
Commitment to Accountability1
During FY 2025-26, PPL reported zero confirmed incidents2 
of child labour, forced labour, discrimination or human rights 
violations involving indigenous communities3 across its 
operations and value chain partners.4 
The Company recognises that protecting human rights, 
promoting inclusion and ensuring fair labour practices are 
essential to organisational resilience, employee engagement 
and long-term sustainable growth. Increasingly, this 
accountability is supported by technology, as we scale the 
use of AI and machine learning across HR operations.
HR Automation through AI and  
Machine Learning
Piramal Pharma Limited advances the use of artificial 
intelligence and machine learning to modernise HR 
operations and strengthen decision-making across the 
organisation. The application of AI and ML is focused on 
improving operational efficiency, enabling predictive insights 
and enhancing experiences for employees, leaders and 
business stakeholders.
Through an integrated HR technology and analytics 
framework, the Company has streamlined HR operations 
by consolidating dashboards to a focused set of key 
performance indicators, migrating analytics capabilities 
to Qlik Cloud and automating multiple HR processes.
Enhancing Employee Experience
Machine learning capabilities have been embedded within 
the HRMS recruitment module to enable more efficient and 
data-driven hiring. These models analyse historical hiring 
data, role requirements and candidate profiles to support 
improved matching and selection. The recruitment journey 
is further strengthened through automated onboarding 
processes that adapt based on data insights, enabling a more 
personalised experience for new joiners.
Automated feedback mechanisms support continuous 
refinement of recruitment and onboarding processes, 
ensuring responsiveness to candidate and employee inputs. 
In parallel, secure and simplified data access enables HR 
teams and business leaders to make timely and informed 
decisions using advanced business intelligence and machine 
learning tools. These same capabilities extend to giving our 
leadership clearer visibility into workforce trends.
Strengthening Leadership Visibility
AI- and ML-enabled reporting provides leadership with 
predictive and forward-looking insights across key 
people metrics. These include headcount trends, learning 
investments, succession readiness, quality of hires, diversity 
indicators, mandatory learning status and attrition forecasts. 
The availability of these insights supports proactive 
workforce planning, strategic alignment and agile  
decision-making across functions and geographies.
Proactive Risk Mitigation
Machine learning models are used to analyse employee 
sentiment, engagement indicators and attrition trends. By 
drawing on feedback data, performance information and 
historical patterns, these models help identify early warning 
signals related to employee satisfaction, engagement and 
retention. This enables HR teams to intervene proactively and 
address potential risks at an early stage. The integration of AI 
capabilities has also strengthened data security by reducing 
the risk of unauthorised data access and sharing.
Seamless System Integration
Given the Company’s global footprint, system consistency 
and data integrity are critical. Machine learning supports 
smooth integration across HR, performance management 
and learning platforms by monitoring and managing data 
flows between systems. This approach ensures timely 
updates, minimises inconsistencies and supports smooth 
HR operations across regions, contributing to a consistent 
employee and stakeholder experience.
Accelerating Intelligent Automation
Automation is being scaled across HR processes through the 
integration of APIs and machine learning algorithms. These 
initiatives have reduced manual effort associated with data 
updates, scheduling, validation and routine operational 
activities across core systems. Progress toward a low-manual 
intervention The HR model allows HR teams to focus more 
effectively on strategic priorities such as talent development, 
leadership planning and workforce engagement, while 
automation manages repeatable operational tasks.

 Sellersville, US
Occupational Health and Safety
Safety is a core priority at Piramal Pharma Limited and is fundamental to 
sustaining operational excellence. Our approach to Occupational Health and 
Safety (OHS) is anchored in a strong belief that every work related injury 
and illness is preventable. We believe in the philosophy that the health 
and well‑being of our workforce form the cornerstone of responsible and 
sustainable operations, while also enabling us to consistently deliver safe, 
reliable and high‑quality products to our customers. This philosophy guides 
how we design systems, engage our people and manage risk across operations 
involving employees, contractors, visitors and surrounding communities.
Our Occupational Health framework is driven by a preventive 
and lifecycle‑based approach, encompassing early risk 
identification, continuous medical oversight and integration 
of statutory requirements with global best practices. Aligned 
with the Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions 
(OSHWC) Code, 2020, our systems are designed to be 
compliant, scalable and future‑ready.
Our Occupational Health model of execution is driven 
through 24x7 Occupational Health Centres (OHCs), supported 
by central governance and integrated digital systems, 
enabling standardised and responsive healthcare delivery 
across locations.
Strengthening the OHS Management System1
PPL’s OHS practices are governed by the Global Environmental, 
Health and Safety (EHS) Policy, applicable to all directors, 
employees, contractors, sub contractors and visitors. The 
policy is supported by six guiding codes and 60 EHS guidelines, 
of which 49 are dedicated to health and safety. To reinforce 
critical safety behaviours and control high risk activities, 12 
mandatory Life Safety Rules (LSRs) are implemented at all 
sites. Compliance with these rules is monitored closely to 
reduce exposure to serious incidents.
Audits continue forming a key pillar of the EHS management 
system. All Indian manufacturing sites are ISO 45001 and 
ISO 14001 certified. ISO audits were in addition to regular 
internal audits, customer audits and regulatory inspections 
to ensure continued adherence to customer, statutory and 
corporate requirements.

===== PAGE 65 =====
Cultivating a Proactive Safety Culture1 
We are focused on embedding safety as an organisational 
value rather than a compliance obligation. This 
transformation is driven through leadership commitment, 
behavioural engagement and employee empowerment.
Leaders across levels reinforce the importance of safety 
through visible involvement, site visits and direct engagement 
with frontline teams. Behaviour based engagement 
encourages employees to speak up, support peers and take 
shared responsibility for identifying and addressing risks. 
Employees are empowered with the authority and tools to 
intervene and stop unsafe work, strengthening accountability 
at the point of operation.
Safety Leadership and Responsibility
The OHS leadership framework is supported by subject 
matter experts in process safety, industrial hygiene and 
occupational health functioning at both site and central 
levels. These leaders translate EHS policies into operational 
practice by embedding safety standards, delivering 
targeted training and engaging with employees, contractors 
and communities.
Systematic follow up on corrective and preventive actions 
ensures closure of gaps and continuous improvement. This 
approach strengthens ownership and supports sustained 
safety performance across the organisation.
Project Lighthouse and Behaviour based Safety
Project Lighthouse serves as our flagship safety culture 
initiative. Originally launched across selected Indian 
manufacturing sites, the programme has been expanded 
across operations and integrated with Leadership Gemba 
Walks in North America and the United Kingdom.
Key elements of the programme include:
	
↗A seven step behavioural intervention framework to identify 
at risk behaviours and reinforce safe practices
	
↗A nine step incident investigation process to enable 
structured learning and effective root cause analysis
	
↗Site specific competence matrices and training plans to 
strengthen frontline capability
	
↗Harmonised safety standards to ensure consistency 
across geographies
Project Lighthouse has reinforced frontline ownership of 
safety. The initiative extends beyond capability building, 
serving as a catalyst for broader cultural change by 
embedding safety awareness into everyday routines, 
decision-making and mind set. Through practical simulations, 
this project facilitates behaviour focused coaching. These 
efforts move past compliance requirements, nurturing 
informed vigilance and reinforcing the belief that every 
individual, regardless of role or designation, contributes to 
a safe, resilient and sustainable workplace.
Key Safety Performance Indicators
	
↗0 fatalities in the last five years
	
↗0.17 Lost Time Injury Frequency Rate (LTIFR-Employees)
	
↗0.54 Lost Time Injury Frequency Rate (LTIFR-Workers)
	
↗2 Total Reportable work-related injuries – Employees
	
↗4 Total Reportable work-related injuries – Workers
	
↗19.34 hours of safety training per employee and worker 
annually across manufacturing sites
	
↗95% CAPA compliance rate

Good practices and initiatives
Green Team Initiative: 
Turning Employee Ideas into 
Environmental Action
Objective and Initiative
To build awareness and capability across the Grangemouth 
site, bespoke sustainability training sessions were 
delivered in partnership with Scottish Enterprise through 
the Net Zero Academy. These sessions engaged both the 
Environment, Health and Safety (EHS) team and the site’s 
cross-functional green team. 
Established as an extension of the Employee Forum, 
the Green Team brings together passionate volunteers 
from across functions who are committed to identifying 
and implementing practical sustainability solutions. 
The team reviews site performance, identifies 
priority areas and aligns initiatives with broader 
environmental commitments.
Workforce Engagement in Health & Safety
Sustained safety performance is reinforced through active 
employee involvement. Platforms such as safety suggestion 
schemes, Kaizen projects and cross functional safety 
committees enable teams to proactively identify hazards and 
drive improvements. Recognition mechanisms acknowledge 
individuals and teams who demonstrate strong safety 
leadership and behaviour.
Awareness campaigns and themed initiatives were conducted 
across locations, including National Safety Week, Fire Safety 
Week and the Safetron Speaks campaign addressing high 
risk topics such as chemical handling, LOTOTO, machine 
guarding and work permit systems. Bite sized digital learning 
modules supported wider reach and engagement across 
employee groups.
Leadership participation in these initiatives reinforced 
the message that safety responsibility begins at the top. 
Safety culture surveys conducted at selected sites provided 
insights into employee perceptions and informed future 
engagement strategies.
Impact
Key initiatives undertaken include replacing 
plastic water cooler cups with paper alternatives, 
introducing dedicated soft-plastic recycling 
stations, organising a site-wide litter collection 
drive to mark World Earth Day and exploring 
the transition from diesel-powered work vans 
to electric vehicles. Through these initiatives, 
employees are actively contributing to reducing 
waste, promoting resource efficiency, and 
supporting the site's decarbonisation journey.

Good practices and initiatives
Creating a Space for Connection, Well-being and Biodiversity
Objective and Initiative
The Grangemouth site is developing a dedicated 
well-being garden designed to provide employees 
with a peaceful space for relaxation, reflection and 
connection with nature.
The initiative is being championed by the site's Mental 
Health First Aiders and Advocate Group, reflecting a 
culture that prioritises psychological well-being alongside 
operational excellence. Funding for the project has 
been raised through employee-led activities, including 
Christmas raffles and smoothie bar events. The garden  
will feature green spaces, planting areas and informal 
seating zones that encourage employees to step away 
from the demands of the working day and recharge in 
a restorative setting.
Good practices and initiatives
Observing Earth Month and 
Safety Week
Objective and Initiative
To mark Earth Month and Safety Week 2025, the 
Sellersville site organised a series of activities focused 
on safety, well-being and environmental responsibility. 
Employees participated in Safety Bingo, shared safety 
improvement ideas, engaged with PPE vendors for on-site 
fittings and demonstrated their commitment through a 
site-wide Safety Pledge. The week also featured wellness 
initiatives, including health screenings, injury prevention 
sessions and stretching demonstrations, culminating in a 
local stream cleanup to support Earth Month.
Impact
The project incorporates sustainability principles 
by promoting biodiversity and embracing circular 
economy practices. Volunteers have committed 
to repurposing wooden pallets generated from 
site deliveries into garden furniture, extending 
the life of materials that would otherwise become 
waste. The well-being Garden exemplifies our well-
rounded approach to creating positive social and 
environmental impact within the workplace.
Impact
The initiative deepened employee engagement, 
reinforced safe workplace behaviours and 
fostered environmental stewardship. Reflecting 
the effectiveness of these efforts, the Sellersville 
site achieved a significant milestone in 
November 2025, completing two consecutive 
years without a lost-time incident.
Good practices and initiatives
Inspiring Future Scientists and 
Safety Leaders
Objective and Initiatives 
As part of its STEM outreach efforts, the site participated 
in a local primary school's "Big Me" careers day in 
Dunipace. Through PPE demonstrations and hazard-
spotting exercises, employees introduced students to the 
role of science and safety in improving patient outcomes. 
The initiative also sparked discussions on expanding 
STEM ambassador programmes and outreach activities, 
a topic further explored at the Scottish Patient Awareness 
Council meeting.

through positive reinforcement,

safety awareness and engagement.

At Dahej
	
↗Above-ground Class A and Class B solvent tanks have been shifted to underground 
tanks to enhance safety and reduce exposure to environmental conditions or

safety awareness sessions that conclude with a safety pledge. Regular training

systematically implemented.
	
↗Fifteen Human Factor (HF)-card points covering key safety measures are 
explained department-wise to build the safety culture.
	
↗LED displays have been installed to communicate and reinforce safety 
measures across the plant. Random checks are conducted to ensure effective 
implementation of safety practices and compliance.
	
↗Signage in the local language is displayed throughout the site for better 
notification of hazards and controls. 
	
↗A CO₂ flooding system is available as part of the fire safety infrastructure.
	
↗The plant has achieved zero harm and zero environmentally hazardous incidents.

At Ennore
	
↗Ennore Safety Park has been established to improve the 
competence and safety awareness of workers. It is located 
away from the plant and includes miniature working models 
of equipment such as centrifuges.
	
↗In three unloading areas, high-risk operations have been 
identified, and lifeline protection systems have been installed 
to improve safety.
	
↗Multiple safety committees are in place, including the 
Contractors’ Safety Committee and department-level safety 
committees, to ensure continuous focus on safety. Line 
departments have taken the responsibility to implement 
Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) and guidelines, 
promoting greater ownership and autonomy.
Tamil Nadu Safety Award – First Prize
Ennore received the first prize at the Tamil Nadu 
Safety Awards in recognition of achieving the 
Lowest Frequency Rate among participating 
industries, reflecting our strong commitment to 
workplace safety.
At Ahmedabad PDS
	
↗There are 18–20 Behaviour-Based Safety (BBS) teams 
present on site, with each team assigned to one department. 
Every team consists of two trained interveners. A BBS-MIS 
(Management Information System) summary is available for 
monitoring and analysis.
	
↗The number of chemicals stored on site has been reduced 
to minimise risks related to fire, explosion and toxic hazards. 
The count has been reduced from around 800 to 200 by 
implementing defined capping limits set by the Site Head.
	
↗Facilities such as a crèche, Occupational Health Centre (OHC),

Process Safety and Operational Reliability
Employee engagement and awareness sit at the core of our 
safety philosophy. Safety is treated as a shared responsibility, 
woven into everyday behaviour and reinforced through 
inclusive participation.
During FY2026, we continued enhancing our Process 
Safety Management (PSM) systems through structured 
maturity assessments and certified training programmes 
for cross functional teams. Process safety considerations 
were integrated into major capital projects at early design 
stages to enable hazard identification and appropriate 
control measures.
The Company aligned with global best practices through 
membership of Centre for Chemical Process Safety, 
enabling access to global frameworks, technical guidance 
and benchmarking tools. We conducted a workshop in 
collaboration with CCPS for creating awareness related 
to chemical compatibility, reactivity and prevention of 
similar hazards supporting consistent application of safety 
measures across sites.
Town halls and open feedback forums promote transparent 
dialogue, while structured recognition programmes 
acknowledge individuals who demonstrate safe behaviours 
that extend beyond basic compliance.
World Environment Day activities focused on tree plantations, 
environmental pledges, and waste‑management awareness, 
reinforcing shared responsibility towards a greener workplace.
Digital learning has complemented on site engagement 
through bite sized e learning modules covering topics 
such as the Work Permit System. Leadership visibility 
during these initiatives has stayed a critical enabler, with 
leaders actively conducting floor walks and recognising 
contributions first hand.
Insights from internal safety culture surveys conducted at 06 
sites in India have further informed engagement strategies, 
enabling more targeted and effective interventions. 
Access to CCPS global standards and best practices 
continues supporting strengthened process safety and 
operational resilience.
Hazard Assessment Process1
To maintain high standards of safety and compliance 
across its operations, PPL has embedded a structured 
Seven Step Hazard Study Process within its risk assessment 
framework. This systematic approach ensures that every new 
product, process or operational change is evaluated with 
rigor. Risks are identified early, understood in depth, and 
addressed through clearly defined control measures before 
implementation.

failures.

risks, ensuring relevance and practical application.

Elevating Contractor Safety Standards
Over the past year, noticeable strides have been made in 
strengthening the way contractor safety is handled across 
all manufacturing locations at PPL. What once operated 
with variations has been brought into closer alignment, 
resulting in shared standards, simpler processes and clearly 
defined expectations. It signals a deliberate move toward a 
more unified and dependable safety environment, one that 
supports both consistency and long-term sustainability.
At the centre of this effort sits a structured Contractor 
Safety Management approach, anchored in our global 
Environmental Health and Safety policy. The system follows 
a six-step framework designed not only to ensure compliance 
but also to actively reduce risk at every stage of contractor 
engagement. Its core elements stay unchanged in intent yet 
have become sharper in execution:
	
↗Pre-qualification to assess readiness and capability
	
↗Induction to familiarise contractors with site requirements
	
↗Training tailored to role and exposure
	
↗Supervision to reinforce safe working conditions
	
↗Incident management to respond and learn effectively
	
↗Performance evaluation to track outcomes 
and improvements
Together, these components continue shaping how 
contractors are integrated into operations. Greater 
importance is now placed on building individual capability 
and encouraging safe behaviour in day-to-day work.
This evolution naturally brings training into sharper focus. It 
is a key driver of improvement, delivered through a clearly 
defined three tier structure that supports both awareness 
and practical execution:
	
↗Basic safety as the starting point for all contractors
	
↗Job specific safety aligned to the nature of assigned roles
	
↗Task oriented training targeting the risks within 
specific activities
By linking structured systems with behavioural development, 
we continue to reinforce contractor safety as a core priority, 
ensuring that progress is both measurable and meaningful.

Occupational Health and Well being1 
A strong foundation in workplace health begins with systems 
that can respond quickly, reliably and at scale. Across all 
our sites, Occupational Health Centres play this critical role. 
Positioned to offer immediate support, these centres act as 
the first point of intervention for health protection, early 
diagnosis and preventive care. Each facility is equipped 
with experienced Factory Medical Officers, trained nurses 
and allied healthcare professionals who ensure consistent 
medical oversight. Their presence strengthens the 
organisation’s ability to address risks early while maintaining 
continuity of care.
Supporting these centres is a broader occupational health 
system that integrates preventive monitoring with structured 
medical oversight. Regular health assessments are conducted 
in line with statutory requirements, while digital records 
are maintained through My Safe to ensure accuracy and 
continuity. The system extends beyond routine care into 
preparedness, with established emergency response 
protocols that enable swift and coordinated action. Key 
elements include:
	
↗Periodic and statutory medical examinations for employees
	
↗Continuous monitoring of workplace health risks with 
preventive controls embedded into operations
	
↗Emergency Response Systems at all locations with defined 
coordinators and clear contact protocols
	
↗24×7 availability of Basic Life Support (BLS) ambulances with 
drivers trained in BLS and defensive driving
	
↗Partnerships with nearby multi-specialty hospitals for swift 
referrals and advanced care access
Preparedness at the ground level further underpins this 
framework. Each department maintains an adequate 
number of trained First Aiders across shifts, aligned with 
legal requirements. Their readiness is supported through 
regular training delivered by certified professionals, ensuring 
confidence in real situations. In parallel, infrastructure 
is maintained with equal rigor, ensuring accessibility 
and reliability:
	
↗Fully stocked First Aid boxes placed across operational zones
	
↗Routine inspections to maintain readiness and compliance
	
↗Scheduled mock drills and emergency simulations to test 
response capability and coordination
While systems form the backbone, the wider impact depends 
on how effectively these practices are understood and 
adopted by the workforce. Programmes are designed with 
accessibility in mind, incorporating site level alignment and 
language sensitive content to engage a diverse contractor 
base. Increasing attention is now directed toward behaviour 
and safety culture, recognising that awareness alone is 
not enough. Contractors are encouraged to internalise 
safety expectations through repeated engagement and 
practical exposure.
This shift is reinforced through structured interactions that 
connect policy with practice:
	
↗Comprehensive inductions that establish expectations from 
the outset
	
↗Toolbox talks that address real time risks and reinforce 
safe habits
	
↗Interactive sessions at the Safety Park to simulate and 
internalise critical controls
	
↗Leadership walkthroughs and joint safety reviews that 
promote shared accountability
We continue to prioritise the physical, mental, and social 
well-being of our workforce through initiatives aligned with 
statutory requirements and international standards. This 
alignment ensures that employees stay informed, supported 
and protected at every stage of employment. Alongside 
preventive healthcare programmes, employees benefit from 
expert-led health awareness sessions promoting healthy 
lifestyles and well-being. These efforts are complemented 
by clean, hygienic, and ergonomically designed workplaces, 
supporting a healthy workforce, improved well-being, and 
sustained productivity.
RoSPA Gold Award
The Grangemouth facility of Piramal Pharma Solutions has 
been honoured with the ‘RoSPA Gold Award’ for a second 
consecutive year by the Royal Society for the Prevention 
of Accidents. The award was based on a comprehensive 
independent review of the site's safety systems, which 
covered key areas such as leadership, key risk prioritisation, 
incident investigation and safety performance monitoring.

to advance site-level sustainability and engagement 
initiatives aligned with our global ESG commitments. 
With changes to ERT support, the site implemented a 
well-structured internal model to ensure continuity of 
emergency preparedness:
	
↗Enhanced reliance on trained first aiders and fire 
wardens across all operational areas.
	
↗Reinforced incident response protocols and 
escalation pathways, maintaining clear linkage with 
external emergency services.
	
↗Continued coordination through established 
emergency contact systems and response channels.
	
↗Ongoing implementation of structured Safety 
Intervention and Engagement programmes to foster 
a proactive safety culture.
This transition has deepened internal ownership of 
safety while maintaining effective coordination with 
external responders. 
As the site underwent significant infrastructure 
changes, a series of strategic adaptations 
were implemented to ensure continued safety 
and compliance. 
	
↗Revised storage and access controls for 
hazardous materials in response to significant 
infrastructure changes.
	
↗Ensured safe and uninterrupted handling of 
flammable and hazardous substances during 
site transitions.
	
↗Maintained compliance with regulatory and 
landlord requirements while strengthening 
operational resilience.

Industrial Hygiene Excellence1
We safeguard the health and well-being of our employees 
through a structured and data-driven Industrial Hygiene (IH) 
programme. We continuously monitor, assess and minimise 
workplace exposures to protect our workforce while 
supporting operational excellence and regulatory compliance.
Aligned with Code 3 of PPL's EHS Management Framework, 
our Industrial Hygiene (IH) programme is anchored in a 
prevention-focused philosophy that emphasises exposure 
reduction and effective control at source, enabled through 
a structured framework of exposure assessment, risk 
evaluation and control verification. 
The mode of execution integrates exposure science, 
toxicological risk assessment, engineering controls, digital 
risk management tools and performance monitoring to 
ensure occupational health risks are systematically identified, 
assessed and controlled.
Our quantitative exposure assessments focus on key 
occupational risks, including:
	
↗Chemical agents and airborne contaminants 
	
↗Noise exposure 
	
↗Ergonomic and biomechanical stressors 
The insights generated support targeted preventive measures 
and stronger workplace controls. As part of our exposure 
monitoring initiatives, site-wide quantitative assessments 
are conducted at key locations including PPDS Ahmedabad, 
Digwal, Dahej and Morpeth, with over 100 personal and 
area samples collected across activities such as charging, 
dispensing, blending, sampling and cleaning. These efforts 
resulted in achieving 100% compliance with Occupational 
Exposure Limits (OELs), supported by effective containment 
systems and appropriate PPE usage.
To further strengthen hazard banding and exposure limit 
setting, we continuously review Occupational Exposure Bands 
(OEBs) for over 120 APIs and intermediates. Occupational 
Exposure Limits (OELs) were derived and verified using 
toxicological data and route-specific No-Observed-Effect 
Levels (NOELs), enabling alignment with international 
standards and supporting safer scale-up and technology 
transfer activities.
Our controls and containment programme include:
	
↗Validation of containment performance for critical equipment 
	
↗Surrogate trials using potent or analogous compounds 
	
↗Strengthening the Respiratory Protection Programme 
through fit testing and APF evaluations 
	
↗Standardisation of PPE selection and usage across India sites 
In Focus: Initiatives at Morpeth
An in-house Industrial Hygiene sampling unit equipped 
with calibrated sampling instruments available at Morpeth 
enhanced our internal monitoring capabilities. This capability 
supports timely exposure monitoring, improved data quality 
and greater responsiveness in managing workplace health risks 
across operations.
We conducted comprehensive industrial hygiene (IH) assessments 
to evaluate employee exposure to physical, biological and 
chemical hazards using both qualitative and quantitative methods.
	
↗Traditional IH techniques, including active and passive sampling 
pumps/media methodologies, were used to assess airborne 
solids, active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs), vapours and gases 
against established occupational exposure limits (OELs). 
	
↗Real-time monitoring equipment, including photo ionisation 
detectors (PID), digital micromanometers and flow hoods, was 
also deployed to assess volatile organic compounds (VOCs), 
airflow velocities, volumes, pressures and the effectiveness 
of local exhaust ventilation (LEV) and general ventilation 
(GV) systems.
	
↗ Furthermore, octave band sound level meters, personal noise 
dosimeter and environmental noise meters were used to assess 
occupational and environmental noise exposure, including the 
impact of continuous site-generated noise during both daytime 
and nighttime operations.
 IH Sampling Pumps and Flow Meters
IH Training and Capability Building
Building internal expertise is a key component of our 
Industrial Hygiene strategy. During the year, selected 
employees participated in Industrial Hygiene training 
programmes conducted by Johnson & Johnson, facilitating 
cross-industry learning, benchmarking of best practices and 
building organisational capability in exposure assessment, risk 
management and occupational health protection. Building this 
expertise also supports our broader commitment to safety 
across plants, processes and transport networks.
Plant, Process and Transport Safety
PPL is committed to safeguarding everyone connected with 
its operations, including Directors, full-time and part-time 
employees, contractors, subcontractors and consultants. This 
commitment covers activities within plants, production areas 
and transportation networks. The Company regularly reviews 
its plant and process safety measures in line with established 
EHS and ESG policies. 
At Piramal Pharma, sustainability is not a side initiative—it is embedded in how we operate. 
Across our sites, we are committed to reducing our environmental footprint, enhancing energy 
efficiency, and driving operational excellence, all while delivering value to our customers and 
communities. Sustainability drives how we operate—efficient, responsible, impactful."
Surinder Gulati  
Executive Vice President – Manufacturing (PPS – India API)
 PDS, India
During the year, systematic ergonomic risk assessments were 
conducted using validated tools such as RULA, REBA and MAC 
to evaluate postural and operational stresses across manual 
handling, packaging and warehouse logistics activities. 
Based on assessment outcomes, engineering redesigns 
and administrative interventions were implemented to 
reduce musculoskeletal disorder (MSD) risks and improve 
workplace ergonomics.
This integrated industrial hygiene strategy reflects our 
commitment to occupational health excellence by embedding 
prevention-focused practices into our EHS governance 
framework and reinforcing our leadership in pharmaceutical 
health and safety performance.

↗Completed site-wide replacement of windows and doors 
to enhance workplace safety and facility integrity.

Safe storage, handling and transportation of 
pharmaceutical compounds stay central to our operating 
discipline. Employees are trained to handle and manage 
hazardous substances safely, while process-level controls 
are continuously strengthened to reduce potential 
risks. A structured governance framework supports 
ongoing monitoring of incident-related risks, enabling 
effective oversight, accountability and corrective action 
management within our operations and value chain.

handling and transportation of hazardous substances.

risk reviews, formal assessments are conducted to 
identify and document environmental risks in line with

prevent workplace accidents, production disruptions, 
chemical exposures, spills and leaks. At our Ennore site, 
lifeline protection system was installed making three 
manual unloading areas and the entire process safer and 
more efficient.

workplace accidents, production disruptions, chemical 
exposures or leaks.

strengthens circularity in operations.

responsible resource use, waste management, biodiversity 
protection and climate action, we continue reducing our 
environmental impact across operations.
By integrating environmental responsibility across the 
value chain, PPL supports a cleaner, safer and more 
resilient future.

Customer Centricity
At Piramal Pharma Limited, customer centricity is not a principle we aspire to; 
it is how we operate. Guided by our core values of Knowledge, Action, Care 
and Impact, every decision across our businesses begins and ends with one 
question: what does the customer need? This approach extends well beyond 
product quality. It encompasses reliability in supply, precision in execution, 
genuine responsiveness and the kind of trust that is built and sustained over 
years of consistent partnership.
Our diversified business model gives us an unusually broad and nuanced understanding of customer expectations across the 
pharmaceutical and consumer healthcare value chain. Through our three core business units, Piramal Pharma Solutions (PPS), 
Piramal Critical Care (PCC) and Piramal Consumer Healthcare (PCH), we address distinct customer segments with tailored 
solutions, strong quality systems and agile execution. Each business operates differently, but all are held to the same standard:  
put the customer at the centre of everything.
By embedding patient centricity into our sustainability journey, we are advancing responsible 
healthcare solutions that create meaningful impact, improve access to care and support healthier 
communities for generations to come"
Stuart Needleman  
Chief Commercial Officer
Three Businesses, One Standard
Piramal Pharma Solutions 
(PPS) serves pharmaceutical 
innovators who demand more 
than a contract manufacturer. 
They need a development 
and manufacturing partner 
who can move with them 
through complexity. PPS 
delivers customised, flexible 
solutions across the full 
drug development and 
manufacturing lifecycle, backed 
by a global footprint, advanced 
technical capabilities and a 
deeply collaborative working 
model. The result is timely 
delivery, high-quality outcomes 
and partnerships built to last.
Piramal Critical Care 
(PCC) operates where the 
stakes are at their highest. 
Serving specialised hospital 
needs in anaesthetics and 
intrathecal therapies, PCC 
combines vertically integrated 
manufacturing across the 
United States and India with 
distribution reaching more 
than 100 countries. This 
breadth of reach and depth of 
capability means that clinicians 
and healthcare systems can 
depend on PCC for the supply 
reliability and precision 
delivery that critical and 
emergency care settings simply 
cannot compromise on.
Piramal Consumer Healthcare 
(PCH) takes a direct and 
dynamic approach to engaging 
the end consumer. Using digital 
platforms, modern trade, 
e-commerce channels and 
targeted communication, PCH 
delivers accessible healthcare 
solutions on a meaningful 
scale. Built on strong, trusted 
brands and an asset-light 
operating structure, PCH 
is designed to stay close to 
evolving consumer needs 
and respond quickly as 
expectations shift.
Measuring What Matters
Commitment to customers must be measurable to be meaningful. We conduct annual customer satisfaction and Net 
Promoter surveys across all business units, and the insights we gather are reviewed systematically and translated into concrete 
improvement plans spanning service delivery, communication quality and the depth of customer engagement. These are not 
benchmarking exercises. They are accountability mechanisms that keep us honest about where we are delivering and  
where we must do better.

and communities we serve.

Caring for the Health and Safety  
of our Customers
Safeguarding customer health and safety is a priority 
across all product categories. We promote transparent 
communication and responsible product use through multiple 
channels, including our website, product documentation and 
Safety Data Sheets. Customer outreach is supported through 
initiatives such as periodic surveys, seminars and structured 
forums, alongside direct customer engagement led by 
senior leadership.
Each manufacturing site hosts a Patient Awareness 
Council consisting of cross-functional employees who 
advocate patient-centric practices and develop targeted 
communication approaches. Our Patient Moments campaign, 
led by these councils, shares real patient stories to build 
employee awareness of the end use impact of their work. 
Driving Patient-Centric Impact  
through PAC Initiatives
Patient Awareness Councils (PACs) across all our sites play a 
vital role in strengthening patient-centric thinking throughout 
our operations and communities. Through awareness 
programmes, stakeholder engagement and feedback-driven 
initiatives, PACs help translate patient needs into meaningful 
actions and outcomes.

Data Privacy and Information Security1
Protecting customer data and maintaining confidentiality 
are critical to sustaining trust. We have implemented a 
multi layered information security framework designed to 
safeguard customer and business information across digital 
platforms. Our systems are routinely updated to address 
emerging vulnerabilities. In parallel, organisational safeguards 
are strengthened through employee awareness programmes 
and data protection training to reinforce responsible 
information handling.
Security measures include antivirus and anti spyware 
solutions, firewalls, secure server environments and regular 
remote data backups. Access to sensitive information 
is governed through strict role-based controls, secure 
authentication protocols and audit trails.
Zero data breaches were reported during the 
reporting period, reflecting the effectiveness of 
our implemented controls. 
Marketing and Labelling2
We ensure that our marketing and product labelling 
practices are governed by a strong framework of regulatory 
compliance, transparency and ethical responsibility. 
Marketing practices are designed to provide accurate, clear 
and meaningful information to stakeholders, with a strong 
focus on patient safety and responsible usage. Product labels 
and accompanying patient information leaflets are developed 
to support informed decision-making and the safe use of 
medicines, while staying aligned with regulatory expectations 
and industry best practices.
All communications adhere to the requirements prescribed 
by Drug Regulatory Authorities in the countries where the 
Company operates, covering detailed standards for product 
and service labelling. 
During FY2026, zero instances of non compliance 
were reported in relation to marketing 
communications, product or service information, 
or labelling standards.3
Brand-building initiatives are undertaken with a consistent 
emphasis on ethical communication and consumer well-
being. Alongside compliance, PPL continues investing in 
building the visibility and equity of key consumer brands such 
as Little’s, Lacto Calamine, i range, Polycrol and Tetmosol.
Accessibility and Affordability
The Company recognises that equitable access to healthcare 
is essential for improving public health outcomes and 
supporting long-term sustainable growth. Improving access 
to healthcare products and ensuring affordability across 
population segments is both a business priority and a social 
responsibility for us.
PPL’s products are made widely available across diverse 
geographies and income groups through a strong and 
expanding distribution network. The Company maintains a 
broad retail footprint across India, with approximately ~180K 
chemists, cosmetics shops and gift stores, complemented by 
a presence in over 8000 modern trade outlets.
In response to shifting consumer preferences and the 
growing importance of digital channels, we have significantly 
strengthened our e-commerce presence. Products are 
available on more than 20 leading e-commerce platforms, 
increasing convenience and reach. In addition, the Company 
operates its own direct-to-consumer platform, Wellify, which 
supports smooth digital engagement and currently processes 
over 8000 consumer orders each month.
We continue expanding into emerging towns and semi-urban 
markets while exploring innovative fulfilment models such 
as last-mile delivery partnerships. These efforts support 
timely product availability and reinforce the Company’s 
commitment to addressing healthcare access gaps. Through 
an integrated approach that combines physical retail 
strength with digital innovation, we work to ensure that 
essential healthcare products stay accessible, affordable and 
responsive to evolving consumer needs.

Our Initiatives with a Focus on System Change1
PPL’s CSR initiatives were designed with a clear focus on 
strengthening foundational education and enhancing 
community well-being. The programme was implemented 
across 112 aspirational districts in India with efforts directed 
toward upgrading government school infrastructure while 
building the capabilities of key stakeholders. 
A wide set of activities formed part of the CSR programme, 
each aimed at reinforcing education systems and supporting 
community participation. The implementation of BaLA 
(Building as Learning Aid) introduced child-centric school 
infrastructure, while school assemblies and Bal Sansad 
(Children’s Parliament) encouraged student engagement in 
governance and learning processes. Strengthening School 
Management Committees (SMCs) and libraries stayed a 
parallel priority, ensuring that institutional support systems 
were reinforced alongside physical upgrades.
Capacity building was central to the programme, with 
structured sessions conducted for master trainers, teachers, 
headmasters and education officials such as CRCs and 
BRCs. These efforts were supported by continuous on-site 
engagement to ensure that new teaching methodologies 
were not only understood but effectively applied in 
classroom settings. Mohalla classes were introduced within 
communities to reach children who had limited access to 
formal schooling, thereby extending learning opportunities 
beyond conventional spaces.
The programme also worked closely with local governance 
bodies, including Sarpanchs and PRI members, by building 
awareness around their responsibilities in education and 
school development. At the same time, public health 
awareness was addressed through training sessions for 
women on safe drinking water practices, which contributed 
to improved health outcomes at the community level.
Aspirational Bharat Collaborative (ABC)
The Aspirational Bharat Collaborative (Bharat Collab), initiated 
by the Kaivalya Education Foundation and Piramal Foundation 
in partnership with NITI Aayog, is a strategic alliance aiming at 
a developed India by 2047. It aims to improve the lives of 100 
Million citizens across 140+ least developed districts, including 
100+ aspirational districts, and covers 170+ aspirational blocks 
as defined by NITI Aayog across India. These districts and blocks 
face significant developmental challenges, including poor socio-
economic indicators, low literacy rates, high dropout rates and 
inadequate infrastructure for quality education.
Focusing on grassroots development through 
decentralisation and local governance, Bharat Collab is 
engaging a broad network of local partners such as PRI 
members, SHGs, faith leaders, local media, NGOs and youth. 
This inclusive model ensures that development is community-
led. Bharat Collab, committed to a collective vision, seeks 
to build an inclusive, empowered and sustainable India, 
inviting all stakeholders to contribute to the vision of a 'Viksit 
Bharat' by 2047.
Impact and Outcomes 
The programme resulted in noticeable improvements in 
classroom engagement and learning outcomes, particularly 
for students from Grades 1 to 5 with a strong emphasis on 
foundational literacy and numeracy. Enhanced child-friendly 
infrastructure played a role in improving student attendance 
while creating a more supportive learning environment.
Educators experienced growth in both competence and 
confidence, allowing them to deliver more effective 
instruction within classrooms. Local governance bodies 
demonstrated increased participation in school development, 
which strengthened accountability and community 
ownership. The initiative also supported health awareness 
efforts and enabled scholarships for students contributing to 
broader developmental outcomes.
All interventions were carried out between April 2025 and 
March 2026 as part of pro bono engagements supported 
through CSR funds. These efforts stayed fully aligned with the 
objective of advancing inclusive and sustainable community 
development across the regions served.

Carbon-Neutral Schools
The Carbon Neutral Schools initiative promotes climate 
action by expanding infrastructure interventions that 
enhance carbon offsetting and sequestration. A carbon 
footprint calculator has been developed and piloted 
across 8 schools in Nuh, enabling baseline assessment of 
emissions across key domains such as energy, water, waste, 
and transport. Early insights have helped identify emission 
hotspots and informed the design of targeted interventions, 
including energy optimisation and improved waste systems. 
This marks an important shift towards data-driven planning 
and measurable outcomes, positioning schools not only 
as centres of learning but also as demonstration units for 
climate action within Panchayats and communities. This focus 
on climate resilience is complemented by a parallel initiative 
on water security at the panchayat level.
Water Secure Panchayat Initiative
Our initiative has been launched to foster community-led 
campaigns and engage citizens in building self-sufficient 
panchayats through effective drinking water management, 
water conservation and greywater management. As part 
of this initiative, over 200 Village Water and Sanitation 
Committees (VWSCs) were activated to lead local water 
efforts, and 2800 VWSC members were trained on planning, 
implementation and monitoring of water resources. 
Additionally, 180 pump operators received training on their 
roles and responsibilities to ensure effective water service 
delivery, and more than 20,000 community members were 
sensitised on safe drinking water practices.

Digwal Story: Education as a Catalyst for Community Transformation
Piramal Foundation for Education Leadership is currently 
implementing two major projects in the Sangareddy 
district, Telangana. The first, driven by the School of 
Climate & Sustainability (SoCS), aims to promote climate-
resilient and sustainable development practices at the 
community level. The second, Vardhana, is a women’s 
skilling initiative led by the School of Gender, with support 
from PPL (Digwal Plant).
School of Climate & Sustainability (SoCS)
Building on efforts from the previous year, SoCS 
expanded its reach to six panchayats (including four new 
additions) across two mandals in Sangareddy district, 
Telangana. The initiative now covers 3,201 households 
and approximately 14,000 people, with 85% belonging to 
marginalised communities.
Key interventions centred on water-resilient agriculture, 
water conservation, solid and liquid waste management 
and women’s livelihoods. These were carried out in 
collaboration with block and frontline functionaries 
from five government departments, PRI representatives 
(Sarpanch/Ward Members), Panchayat secretaries and 
technical partners such as Krishi Vigyan Kendra.
Impact during the Year:
Farmer engagement
Four Gandhi Fellows and four Karuna Fellows, along with 
the Foundation team, conducted awareness campaigns 
for 300 farmers on soil testing, soil health cards and 
Rabi crop planning.
Waste management
Campaigns in schools encouraged waste segregation, 
composting and the reuse of greywater for 
kitchen gardens.
Institution strengthening
Newly elected PRI members and Panchayat secretaries 
from 6 panchayats were orientated on Village Water 
and Sanitation Committee (VWSC) roles, with support 
provided to establish new committees, ensuring women’s 
representation.
Education and youth development
Around 40 girls from a madrasa in Digwal received training 
in digital literacy and English communication. Additionally, 
75 children accessed computer education through 
Community Learning Centres, 5 Anganwadi workers were 
supported to enhance pre-school education for 100 
children and 2 school libraries were set up, benefiting 
over 150 children.
Vardhana 
Project Vardhana was initiated in November 2024 in 
collaboration with Piramal Pharma Limited (PPL), Digwal, 
with the objective of strengthening women’s agency and 
enhancing their participation in the workforce, particularly in 
the manufacturing sector.
Since its inception, the Skill Development Centre at Digwal 
has trained 139 Sanginis, local young women aged 20 to 27, 
from 5 mandals and 20 gram panchayats in the Sangareddy 
district. Of these, 112 were trained during the reporting 
period, with 75% belonging to marginalised communities, 
including minorities. The Sanginis underwent six structured 
immersions designed to build market-relevant skills and 
sharpen professional competencies.
As a result, 72% of the trained Sanginis have secured career 
opportunities with reputed organisations such as MRF, 
Mahindra, Piramal Pharma, CBM Foundation and Stream 
Digital Services.
The Skill Development Centre has also gained recognition 
and established partnerships with over 20 stakeholders 
across industry, academia, and government departments, 
broadening its ecosystem for scale, impact and excellence. 
The impact of this programme is best captured through the 
experience of one participant' journey.
Gousiya’s Journey
Gousiya’s childhood was shaped by resilience and sacrifice. Despite excelling 
in her studies, due to several barriers she was forced to discontinue her 
education early. 
With newly acquired skills and renewed confidence, she secured a position at 
MRF, taking a strong step toward financial independence and personal growth.

The School of Climate & Sustainability Success 
Stories
In rural India, decisions about water are shaped every day – 
when a farmer chooses how much to irrigate, when a Gram 
Panchayat sets its annual priorities and when households 
form habits around waste and sanitation. The School of 
Climate and Sustainability is built on the belief that lasting 
water security emerges only when these choices evolve. Such 
change is possible when communities, government systems 
and scientific knowledge move in step, guiding decisions that 
protect water resources for generations to come together. 
In FY2026, the School of Climate & Sustainability (SoCS) 
worked across Sehore in Madhya Pradesh, Ranchi in 
Jharkhand, Telangana, Maharashtra and Jammu & Kashmir, 
weaving together three streams of work:
	
↗Helping farmers use water more efficiently in their fields
	
↗Strengthening the conservation structures and governance 
systems that determine how much water is available
	
↗Improving the quality of soil and water through better 
waste management
This work reached over 1,500 farmers and community 
members and deepened collaboration with key government 
departments and programmes, including the Integrated 
Watershed Management Programme, Jal Jeevan Mission, 
Soil and Water Conservation Department, Swachh Bharat 
Mission, Watershed Department, Panchayati Raj Department, 
Ministry of Agriculture and Ministry of Jal Shakti, helping 
advance efforts in water conservation, sustainable 
agriculture, sanitation and rural development.

programme. Our efforts to improve soil and water quality 
focused on building technical capacity, enhancing monitoring 
systems and enabling evidence-based decision-making 
across states. 
In Maharashtra, over 120 block officials were trained 
in collaboration with the Department of Water Supply 
and Sanitation (under the Ministry of Jal Shakti) and the 
Swachh Bharat Mission (Gramin) ecosystem, while targeted 
leadership training supported effective implementation 
of water and sanitation programmes. To strengthen water 
quality management, verification surveys of electro-
chlorination units were conducted across 10 villages. 
Additionally, 18 surveys have been done so far to encapsulate 
the status of solid liquid waste management. Piramal 
Foundation is also a partner in RWPF (Rural WASH Partners 
Forum) with the Ministry of Jal Shakti. 
In Jammu & Kashmir, capacity-building initiatives reached 
over 80 officials across the Public Health Engineering 
Department (PHED), Department of Rural Development, 
Department of Agriculture, and Department of Horticulture, 
including specialised training on water quality challenges 
such as ammoniacal nitrogen contamination. System-
level improvements were further supported through 
sanitation and Swachhata Green Leaf Rating (SGLR) 
surveys across tourism facilities, in partnership with the 
Department of Drinking Water and Sanitation, Ministry 
of Jal Shakti, generating actionable insights and enabling 
the identification of model sites for improved waste and 
sanitation management.
Good practices and initiatives
From Chemicals to Confidence: Advancing Soil  
Health and Crop Resilience in Sehore
Shri Anup Verma, a farmer from Amajhir village in Sehore 
district, Madhya Pradesh, was previously reliant on 
chemical fertilisers and pesticides for onion cultivation. 
The onions produced had a short storage life, resulting in 
significant post-harvest losses.
Through continuous field-level support from the 
agriculture extension team, he transitioned to 
incorporating Good Agricultural Practices (GAP), 
Integrated Pest Management and Nutrient Management. 
Chemical inputs were replaced with locally prepared 
natural solutions such as Amrit Khad and neem-based 
formulations, along with the use of sticky traps and 
Trichoderma for pest control. He implemented these 
practices on 40 decimals of land for onion cultivation 
and subsequently expanded to wheat cultivation 
across one acre.
The transition resulted in improved soil health, better 
moisture retention and a significant reduction in pest 
attacks. Crop quality improved, and naturally grown onions 
demonstrated enhanced storage life, staying unspoiled for 
extended periods. Encouraged by these outcomes, Shri 
Verma scaled these practices and began influencing other 
farmers in his community to adopt similar approaches. His 
efforts have been formally recognised at the district level, 
and he has further strengthened his knowledge through 
advanced training.

From Hesitation to Leadership: Urmila Devi’s  
Journey toward Safe Water Access
In Paika village of Angara block, Jharkhand, Urmila Devi’s 
journey reflects determination shaped by lived experience. 
Growing up in poverty and witnessing persistent water 
scarcity, she was driven to improve both her family’s 
circumstances and her community’s well-being. She has 
been actively involved in water, sanitation and hygiene 
initiatives, mobilising community participation since 
2013. Urmila, a Jalsahiya (a female community mobiliser 
appointed by the Gram Panchayat to oversee water 
quality, sanitation and hygiene), received structured 
training and hands-on support that gradually built her skills 
and confidence, enabling her to independently test water 
sources and ensure regular monitoring. She soon extended 
her impact by training other Jalsahiyas and raising 
awareness about safe water practices within her village. 
Today, Urmila plays a proactive role in improving local 
water management systems, contributing to improved 
water quality monitoring and community awareness.
Good practices and initiatives
Natural Farming Takes Root in Sehore
Rising chemical input costs, degraded soil from years of 
pesticide uses and no local access to organic alternatives 
led to farmers facing low, unsustainable yield. Due to lack 
of resources and heavy monetary implications, sustainable 
farming was a distant aspiration for them. 
SoCS along with the Green Farm BRC Hub established one 
Bio-Input Resource Centre (BRC) per village across five 
villages in the Sehore district to provide aid and relief in 
this situation. Each centre is run by a progressive farmer 
selected and trained to locally produce organic inputs, 
including Jeevamrit, bio-pesticides and Panchpati Kadha. 
They then distribute them to the surrounding farming 
community at a fraction of market cost. The model is 
rooted in the principle of दे खो, सुनो, सीखो (see it, hear it, 
and learn it), turning each BRC into a living demonstration 
site where farmers observe, learn and replicate natural 
farming practices. 
Today five BRCs serve over 600 farmers across 625 acres. 
Organic inputs produced locally have reduced input costs 
by 50–60%, easing the economic burden that had long 
made sustainable farming unviable for smallholders. With 
reduced chemical use, soil fertility is being restored across 
participating farms. Each centre trains approximately 125 
farmers annually, building a self-sustaining network of 
natural farming knowledge anchored within the community.

members in Ranchi
Good practices and initiatives
Bridging Systems and Communities: Shri Gulab Ahirwar’s  
Path to Strengthening Rural Development
In Sehore district of Madhya Pradesh, Shri Gulab Ahirwar’s 
work as an Assistant Programme Officer (APO) under 
MGNREGA reflects a deep commitment to social welfare 
and grassroots development. With over 17 years in the 
field, he gained experience across multiple blocks. He has 
consistently sought to uplift marginalised communities by 
bridging administrative systems with on-ground realities. 
Shri Ahirwar collaborated with the PF team to strengthen 
water conservation efforts. By equipping GRSs with the 
skills to lead water conservation and Solid & Liquid Waste 
Management (SLWM) efforts under the Gram Panchayat 
Development Plan (GPDP), he helped drive measurable 
progress. Supported by Gandhi Fellows, the Sehore 
block witnessed significant achievements, including the 
construction of 454 farm ponds, the creation of 785 dug 
wells and the rejuvenation of 44 ponds, largely enabled by 
a network of 64 active Jaldoots.
Looking ahead, he aims to scale these efforts across 30 
Gram Panchayats in the Sehore district by using MGNREGA 
resources and influencing GPDP fund allocation in 
collaboration with fellows and the PF team. Over his 17 
years in the field, he has gained a greater understanding 
of the system, while the constant support of our fellows 
has helped him to gain trust in the system. Through 
continuous encouragement in his journey, he has moved 
from a highly individual-driven approach to recognising 
the value of trust, teamwork and collaboration. This shift 
has deepened his leadership and reinforced his belief 
that collective action and open communication are 
essential to overcoming systemic challenges and achieving 
sustainable impact.
Strengthening Employee Volunteering
Change at this scale is not driven by community members 
alone,  our own employees play an active role too. Employee 
participation is a key enabler of our community engagement 
efforts. To strengthen leadership alignment with social 
priorities, the Piramal School of Leadership ensured newly 
joined senior leaders from India participated annually in 
the Community Immersion Programme, which offers direct 
exposure to the Piramal Foundation’s work alongside district 
administrations and local communities. During FY 2025-26, 
senior leaders contributed approximately 150 volunteering 
hours, gaining firsthand insight into grassroots realities and 
consolidating values-based leadership.
Beyond leadership immersion, employees across sites 
volunteered across four thematic areas: Environment, 
Healthcare, Education and Youth Development and 
Livelihoods. Collectively, employees contributed 
approximately 1,500 volunteering hours, reinforcing a culture 
of empathy, participation and purpose-driven engagement.
1,500+ Hours
Contributed by employee volunteers 
across community initiatives
Enhancing Local Community Bonds
PPL sites actively participate in local community engagement 
through transparent communication and inclusive initiatives. 
Partnerships with NGOs supporting children with diverse 
abilities provide platforms for creative expression, skill 
development and confidence building, while also fostering 
empathy and awareness among employees.
Across all community initiatives, PPL is guided by the belief 
that sustainable development must be inclusive, locally 
rooted and collaborative. Through continued investment, 
partnerships and employee participation, the Company seeks 
to enable communities to thrive with dignity, opportunity 
and resilience.

Risk
Global warming, 
greenhouse gas emissions 
and climate change 
continues to intensify 
risks, making climate

essential to address

and mitigate long-term 
business risks.
We have undertaken 
a detailed exercise 
to strengthen our 
decarbonisation strategy to 
mitigate the risk. We have 
initiated to switch to agro-
briquettes and renewable 
energy.
Positive implication 
due to process 
improvement, 
energy efficiency and 
improved stakeholder 
output.
2
Patient Safety 
and Product 
Quality
Risk
Product quality and 
patient safety are 
fundamental to regulatory 
compliance, patient well-
being and stakeholder 
confidence. Maintaining 
robust quality standards 
is essential to mitigate 
operational, financial and 
reputational risks.
We are on a quality 
advancement journey from 
'Quality for Compliance' to 
'Quality as a Culture', with a 
focus on systems, processes, 
technology and people. We 
have a dedicated Corporate 
Quality Assurance Group 
which actively monitors 
adherence to the prescribed 
product quality standards.
Potential positive 
implication due 
maintenance of 
customer loyalty and 
long-term strategic 
partnerships.

Risk

and stricter waste 
management regulations 
require responsible 
handling to minimise

and compliance risks.
We have enhanced waste 
segregation, recycling, 
resource recovery and 
responsible disposal 
practices while promoting 
circularity across operations.
Negative implication 
due to higher waste 
management and 
disposal costs, 
regulatory penalties, 
environmental 
liabilities and 
reputational risks.

the continuous

Established robust guiding

for Board members, senior

commitments to ethical 
and transparent business 
practices.
Negative implication 
due to cost incurred 
on systems and

strict compliance with 
applicable/ emerging 
regulations.

INDEPENDENT VERIFICATION STATEMENT 
to the Management of Piramal Pharma Limited 
 
Piramal Pharma Limited (Corporate Identity Number U24297MH2020PLC338592, hereafter referred to as ‘PPL’ or ‘the Company’) 
has commissioned DNV Business Assurance India Private Limited (“DNV”, ”us” or “we”) to conduct an independent verification of 
its corporate Greenhouse Gas (‘GHG’) emission data (for the scope 1, scope 2 and selected categories under the scope 3)                                       
for the period 01/04/2025 to 31/03/2026 (FY 2025-26). 
 
 
Emissions Scope 1 
Total GHG Emissions (MT CO2e) for FY 2025-26 
Scope 1 GHG emissions 
47,796 
Scope 2 GHG emissions (location-based) 
92,626 
Scope 2 GHG emissions (market-based) 
62,412 
Scope 3 GHG emissions 
 6,48,835  
 
Scope of Work and Boundary 
The scope of work as agreed with PPL included a limited level of assurance of the 
GHG emissions comprising of Scope 1, Scope 2 and Scope 3 emissions data for   
FY 2025-26. Verification of GHG emissions from the Company’s operations, 
comprising of: 
•
Scope 1 GHG emissions : Covering combustion of fossil fuels and other emissions, 
such as Combustion of coal, Diesel (HSD & LSD), fuel oil, furnace oil, natural gas and 
Liquified Petroleum Gas (LPG) and fugitive emissions including refrigerant refilling in 
air conditioners. 
•
Scope 2 GHG emissions: Covering the GHG emissions on account of purchased 
electricity and purchased steam. 
•
Scope 3  GHG emissions: Other Indirect GHG emissions (Scope 3 emissions) arising from 
value chain covering thirteen categories as per the Greenhouse Gas Protocol’s Corporate 
Value Chain (Scope 3) Accounting which includes  
•
Category-1: Purchased Goods and Services 
•
Category-2: Capital Goods  
•
Category-3: Fuel-and-energy-related activities  
•
Category-4: Upstream Transport & Distribution  
•
Category-5: Waste Generated in Operations  
•
Category-6: Business Travel  
•
Category-7: Employee commute  
•
Category-8: Upstream Leased Assets  
•
Category-9: Downstream transportation and distribution 
•
Category-10: Processing of Sold Products  
•
Category-11: Use of Sold Products  
•
Category-12: End-of Life Treatment of Sold Products  
•
Category-15: Investments  
Reporting and verification boundary covers the GHG emissions from all the 
Piramal Pharma’s operations that fall under the direct operational control of the 
Company’s Legal structure. Based on the agreed scope with the Company, the 
boundary of limited assurance covers the operations of Piramal Pharma across 
all global locations covering sixteen manufacturing plants (India – 09, USA – 04, 
UK – 02 & Canada – 01) and one Corporate / Head Office (India).

Basis of our conclusion 
DNV planned and performed the verification assessment to obtain the necessary 
evidence to provide limited level of assurance for Scope 1, 2 and 3 GHG emissions 
while adopting a risk-based approach in selecting samples to assess the robustness 
of the underlying data management system, information flow, controls, quality 
verification, and check procedures. DNV carried out the following activities: 
•Desk review of the Scope 1, 2, and 3 emissions for the period from 01/04/2025 to 
31/03/2026. Sampling of activity data for verification in line with the requirements for 
verification.  
•Understanding 
the 
GHG 
management 
procedures, 
including 
formats, 
assumptions, emission factors, and calculation methodologies, as well as the 
Company’s GHG data management processes used to generate, aggregate, and 
report the GHG data, assessing completeness and accuracy. 
•Site verifications involving on-site and remote audits in corporate office and selected 
sites as listed out in Annexure II as part of the process of reviewing the Company's 
internal protocols, processes, controls related to the collection & collation of its GHG 
emissions assertions and for verifying the identified activities & GHG emission sources 
& related evidence at the site level on a sample basis.  
•Interactions with key managers and data owners to review data consolidation 
systems related to the GHG inventory, including reviews of emission factors and 
assumptions used in the calculation methodology. 
•Evaluation of GHG emissions data using the reliability principle in conjunction with PPL’s 
methodologies (which are based on GHG Protocol) on data analysis, aggregation, 
measurement, and reporting. 
•Verification of the calibration status of equipment being used to monitor and generate 
activity data on a sample basis. 
Reporting Criteria and Verification Standards 
PPL has prepared its GHG inventory data in reference to the requirements of GHG 
Protocol: A Corporate Accounting and Reporting Standard. 
DNV has carried out this customized engagement in accordance with the principles 
and requirements as per ISO 14064-3:2019. This provides a limited level of 
assurance on PPL’s GHG performance data based on the principles of Relevance, 
Completeness, Consistency, Transparency, and Accuracy applying a ±5% 
materiality threshold for errors and omissions. 
 
Responsibility of the Company 
PPL is responsible for the collection, analysis, aggregation, calculations and 
presentation of data and information related to GHG emission data assertions                   
(based on methodologies defined in Greenhouse Gas Protocol–                                    A 
Corporate Accounting and Reporting Standard) by adopting the ‘operational control’ 
model as a performance data consolidation approach. 
 
DNV’s Responsibility 
Our responsibility for performing this work is to the management of PPL only and in 
accordance with the scope of work agreed with the Company; however, this statement 
represents our independent opinion. The verification engagement assumes that the 
data and information provided to us are complete, sufficient, and true. DNV disclaims 
any liability or co-responsibility for any decision a person or entity would make based 
on this verification statement. 
 
For DNV Business Assurance India Private Limited,
